ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e108	Honourable co-representatives! On 3 December 2017, former depositor Kjell Opseth from Førde died, 81 years old. He represented the Labour Party and Sogn and Fjordane for five periods, from 1981 to 2001. During this time he was among others. Member of the Energy and Industry Committee, Secretary of the Transport Committee and Secretary of the Trade Committee. From 1990 to 1997, he served for six years as Minister of Transport in Gro Harlem Brundtland's Third Government and for a year as Minister of Local and Labour in the Government of Jagland. There are very few who experience being referred to as a legend in their lifetime. But Kjell Opseth was one of these. He was a symbol of political action. He was not the one who gleaned on the speaker's chair with great visions and invigorating wording. Instead, he was the man who carried out, where others talked. Of what he said, we think best of the short, subtle comments, which only strengthened the image of what the English call "no-nonsense" – no nonsense. There is no lack of physical monuments after Opseth's political function. He was the one who cut through and obtained multiple numbers in the seemingly eternal battles over the main airfield of the Austland and the Oslo–Bergen route. Oslo airport Gardermoen is now located, as one of the world's most beautiful and well-functioning airports, and Gardermobanen likeins, with its efficient transport of passengers into and out of the capital. And in his dear homeland lies the Laerdal Tunnel – still the longest in the world – and arouses respect, gratitude and some mild smiles, including among those who have never run it, but remembers Opseth and the political craftsmanship. Another monument is less attentive, but so big: the adventure of Telenor. With Opseth, the old Telecom was transformed into what has been a locomotive in Norwegian business and technology development, a significant contributor to our common well-being, one of the world's ten largest mobile operators. Behind the impression he gave, and the background he came from, the modernizing, national community builder may have come a little in the shadow. Because that's what Kjell Opseth was -- a national community builder. Behind the particular characteristics such as results-oriented, solution-oriented and pragmatic, there may also be something else in the shadow: his commitment and deeper, social-democratic justice understanding of what it is all about, building a country for the benefit of future generations, building an entire country, feeding home, workplaces and futures in all parts of the country, and then tying these parts together to a whole – to the community we are and should be. We shine peace over Kjell Opseth's memory.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e387	The Committee has considered Prop. 146 L for 2016-2017 and released its suspension. The legislative proposal is based on the provision of a basis for loss of citizenship as a result of criminal offences and on the vital interests of the nation. This matter has been dealt with in depth several times. The proposal concerns new § 26(a) and § 26(b) of the Civil Code. Paragraph 26(a) deals with the loss of citizenship as a result of criminal offences under the Criminal Code chapters 16, 17 and 18 with a penalty framework of up to six years or more. These infringements are considered to be highly detrimental to the vital interests of the nation, which is a criterion for the loss of citizenship. The majority of the Committee supports the Government's proposal for the loss of citizenship on the basis of criminal offences. The Committee has been consulted that the loss of citizenship under this provision is to be regarded as a criminal offence and must be decided upon in court. Paragraph 26(b) deals with the loss of citizenship for the vital interests of the nation. The Government proposes a basis for a person to lose his or her citizenship if, in his or her conduct, he or she is considered to be a serious threat to the vital interests of the nation. In this case, the person need not be convicted. This home is intended as a prevention measure. The government does not regard this as a punishment and wishes for the need to act quickly and to avert any terrorist attacks etc. loss of citizenship under this provision should be carried out administratively. The decision may not be appealed, but the case may be brought before the Court of Justice for a final judgment, where the State bears all the costs. The majority of the Committee does not wish to introduce administrative grounds for withdrawal of citizenship. There is a proposal from the majority that the government should come back with a case that, for whatever reason, the loss of citizenship should be decided by the courts. I assume that the various parties hereafter will elaborate their views on the matter. It seems strange that the majority do not want to introduce a law that allows rapid action to prevent terrorism, through administrative procedures. Legal certainty is an important principle for all parties, and sometimes we all need to set aside some preventive measures to ensure legal certainty, sometimes vice versa. This is a constant balancing exercise, taking into account the legal certainty of potential terrorists and criminals against the security of the nation and its citizens. In this case, it is completely wrong to put legal certainty before the proposed safety measure. The legal certainty of this proposal has been adequately ensured. Other countries in Europe have such provisions, and they have used them. The European Court of Human Rights has dealt with a case from England, and the conclusion is that human rights are right. Those concerned will be given the opportunity to refer the matter to the court afterwards, where the State covers all costs. Based on this, the need to protect us from terrorism in this matter must be given priority first. A Norwegian citizenship will give a potential terrorist more benefits in planning an attack. People who are thought to be such a big threat, we simply don't want them here in the country. That's why we have to take their citizenship and get them expelled. In order to avert a possible terrorist attack, it is obvious that it must be acted quickly, we cannot wait for several months for a judgment in such a situation. In Norway, there are many people who have dual citizenship, but fortunately it will be extremely few that will include this home. The PST suggests that today there may be five to ten people who are considered to be such a serious threat. PST has also said that in a few cases they have been without action and room for action in relation to people who are considered to be a serious threat. The limited potential impact suggests that the measure is targeted. Someone says that since so few can be hit, there's an unnecessary warrant. I will strongly warn against this argument. If such a permit can only avert a single attack, the home is vital to its adoption. The Progress Party will include the proposals we are included in the vote. I hope for a good and factual debate on this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e436	The purpose of this matter is to secure the Norwegian community. The aim is to prevent terrorist attacks on Norwegian soil. The aim is to find the right means to do this. Today, we are examining the proposals of the Ministry of Justice and Security to introduce provisions of the Civil Code on the loss of citizenship in the event of criminal offences for fundamental national interests. The discussion this past week has not been about it, but about the Minister of Justice Facebook. The reactions have been many and strong, and the Workers' Party has felt particularly affected. The Prime Minister, on behalf of the Government, has complained about the post. The Minister of Justice has said she wants to apologize. As a magnification representative from the Right, I am pleased with that, but I hope now that the focus can also be focused on the discussion on how to combat radicalisation and terror on Norwegian soil. We tolerate a strong discussion of the instruments in the matter of combating terrorism, as in other cases. New measures are needed in the government's action plan against radicalisation and violent extremism. It was submitted in 2014. One of the new proposals is measures on the provision of pensions for the loss of citizenship in the event of criminal offences. It is then a condition that the person has shown a behaviour that is very damaging to Norwegian vital interests. The possibility of loss of citizenship by criminal offences in the interests of fundamental national interests is believed to have a preventative effect in the Right and, in our opinion, will contribute to increased security in Norway. It appears from the bill that the loss of citizenship for reasons of fundamental national interest is not classified as a penalty under the European Court of Human Rights or under the Constitution. The Council of Europe Convention provides that citizenship, inter alia: may be deprived of persons whose conduct is strongly detrimental to the vital interests of the nation or of persons participating in a foreign military force. We call it foreign fighters in Norway. The majority of the Committee supports the introduction of a legal basis for the loss of citizenship by punishment, following a provision with a penalty framework of up to six years or more. Depriving someone from a citizenship is serious, and it requires a particular good reason. It is a majority to be able to deprive persons of Norwegian citizenship in the interests of fundamental national interests. The substance of the dispute and the reasons for the disagreement in this matter are the proposal of the Government that the loss of citizenship may be adopted administratively. The right considers that the importance of efficiency and the availability of rapid action in a critical situation in order to avert a terrorist attack speaks for it. Investigation and judicial review of criminal offences are often time-consuming. The Courts of Justice may fully review the Ministry's assessment of the serious damage or serious damage to Norway caused to the specific act. In the United Kingdom, they have a similar system under which administrative decisions on the loss of citizenship are taken. They have also been tried in the European Court of Human Rights in 2017, where a citizenship was deprived of a terrorist offence. In order to avert a terrorist attack, police authorities need effective tools. Depriving citizenship would be relevant in very special cases and where the person concerned has a appearance that is detrimental to the interests of Norway. The proposal for the administrative loss of citizenship will not be given a majority today, but the Right and the Progressive Party have, in the proceedings in Stortinget, made a proposal to the Government to determine whether a system of prompt judicial proceedings can be established on matters relating to the loss of citizenship in the interests of fundamental national interests. We hope that parties can support this, and thereby invite the other parties to join in supporting such an investigation.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e459	Let me first attach a few words to the matter itself. This is also a matter where the government actually gets a majority to lower the penalties from 21 to 6 years in terms of the kind of possible loss of citizenship. There's one single little point where one separates, and that's the question of how this is going to happen. The majority support the assessment given in NOU 2015: 4, that the loss of citizenship is subject to judicial review, while the government and the minority of the Committee want the loss of citizenship to take place administratively. ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e519	The biggest task is the welfare of citizens, and the security of every country should be its main task. There are many instruments that politicians can use to achieve this goal, yet we politicians say no to the use of several such instruments. You weigh and weigh different considerations before you land on the right balance. For example, while some people may feel that total monitoring provides more certainty, no court states are in favour of this because they do not want to completely deprive their citizens of their privacy. In the matter under consideration, the crux of the dispute is whether or not the politicians of the Storting want to deprive some criminals and persons who threaten fundamental national interests, their citizenship or not. Everyone agrees that this should be possible, provided that the conditions are met. Everyone agrees. What you disagree about, for example, is: SV wants a 21-year limitation period for criminality. The Right Party and the Progressive Party want the citizenship to be abolished administratively, while the other six of us, the overwhelming majority of the Grand Duchy, that is, all parties except the Right Party and the Advance Party, believe that it is a judge who should be able to do so. That we, that is, all political parties on the Great Britain except the Right and the Advance Party, believe that a judge should decide this, makes us neither blue-eyed nor naive. Nor do we run the terrorists' errands because we want legal proceedings. Nor is it that we or the Workers' Party put the security of terrorists before Norway's security. It's a totally untrue, unacceptable and irresponsible claim. It has been aimed at the Workers' Party, but it has nevertheless been important for me as a Left-Man to say publicly that I have never met a single politician in my life, either from the Workers' Party or from other parties in Norway, who wants to put terrorist security ahead of Norway's security. It is good that both the Prime Minister, the Minister of Knowledge and Integration – and the Minister of Justice, I look at the newspaper.no – now regrets and apologizes. We teach our children to apologize when you do something wrong, and it should not be felt as a major political defeat to apologize – it should be a very good thing to do, and it is good that it is done. Although this famous Facebook post was aimed at the Workers' Party, it might as well have been aimed at several parties here, because it is not just the Workers' Party that requires legal proceedings. We in the Left, the third party of government, have been very clear about the demand for judicial redress. It goes without saying that we cannot have a situation in which the most intrusive action a country can take away from citizenship and, with it, permanently expel a person from Norway and Schengen, can be done administratively. It will erode the point of being a state, the security of citizens – all citizens’ security, including those who may lose their citizenship. Until one is convicted of some criminal offence, until it has been demonstrated that one poses a threat to fundamental national security and national interests, politicians should continue to be equally concerned with the legal certainty of every citizen. I am a former defense lawyer and a police lawyer, and I learned from law school: everyone must be considered innocent in a criminal sense until he is convicted. There is one of the most fundamental things in a democracy, in a state of law. It's the actual bearer in the state of law. The most intrusive measure, to be permanently expelled, we believe in the Left to be decided by an independent court. That should be completely obvious. I would very much like to hear the current Council of State Jan Tore Sanner, who has this as their constitutional responsibility, say something concrete about the following: Does the Council of State understand that we other parties believe that legal proceedings ensure our rule of law? Does the Council of State see that with judicial proceedings, we are ensuring exactly what we are set to do – ensuring the security of our citizens? Will he ask his party, the Right, and possibly the Advance Party, to vote in favour of this subsidiary? It should not be necessary to clarify that, of course, we are all concerned with combating terrorism and taking action to prevent terrorist acts, but I do. We can do this with a court of law, like a very fast one. We in the Left will support the investigation of a rapid court, because it should not be such that a matter remains pending for years. The courts may make this assessment – a proportionate and reasonable assessment – and have the necessary confidence to do so. We must stand together in the fight against terrorism. We must be able to say that we are all concerned about taking action to prevent terrorism. In order to combat terrorism, we need effective action, and we need to stand together politically rather than suspect the motives of other parties. Finally, this provision alone is not going to solve the main problem, how we fight extremism and terror, because it has to do a very long integration work, for example, but we also need to have good intelligence and criminal prosecution. Finally, I would like to say: it must not come to you that the only punishment, for example. Norwegian foreign fighters get, is to be deprived of citizenship in order to keep them out. Being held responsible for their criminal acts, having to stand ready for this, being judged, must be the first track. The next step must be citizenship, but the loss of citizenship should not prevent us from being held responsible for war criminals.

Two persons with identical family ties may be associated with Norway, one of whom, in accordance with the requirements of the Norwegian authorities, has renounced a previous citizenship in order to obtain Norwegian citizenship, while the other still has two nationalities. Only the latter, on the government's proposal, will be able to be deprived of citizenship. In addition, the police already have the possibility of withdrawing passports for people they fear will commit crime while travelling abroad. Police may also detain persons who have committed or are planning terrorism. In addition, we know that there are very few people who have double citizenship in Norway, that the number of people with double citizenship who are potential terrorists is even lower, and that the number of potential terrorists with double citizenship who can be captured by the police and lose citizenship is very close to zero. Is the threat greatest from those who receive punishment and rehabilitation here to land, or can the motive for revenge increase from those who are in another country after being deprived of citizenship? Since there is no majority of administrative decisions in these cases, the Progress Party and the Right have also made proposals to investigate a rapid judicial procedure. It's my hope that a majority can join that compromise.

We believe that this can only happen in court. When the Right and Advance Party want the job as both actor, jury and judge, the Stortinget says no. A majority of the Grand Duchy does not wish to grant such powers. Judicial States do not fall into one piece. Those that fall are bit by bit. Taking administrative decisions on citizenship is such a piece. What the Right Party and the Advance Party are proposing is to make our rule of law less, so that it will be easier to defend. They obviously don't see the irony in that they're building down what they say they're building up. The size must be more principleal and long-term in its assessment of this matter than the government turns out to be. The majority of the Grand Duchy does not agree that it is the administration of the Government which is required to take decisions on the loss of citizenship. It's too intrusive. The representative of Engen-Helgheim has on several occasions compared this to the fact that the police can take away your driving licence. It's very unjealous. Citizenship has entered the heart of the nation-state. It's a visible proof of who you are, and where you belong, a sign that you carry the rights managed in this room, the right to vote in elections, to go in and out of the realm. Depriving someone of a citizenship is a very radical act. It is an action that we must take only when it is absolutely necessary – and right. The Court of Justice is the instrument of the rule of law. If you're in Norway planning terror, and the police think it's a probability balance that you want to implement the plans, you'll be put behind lockdown and hit. Whether or not you are deprived of your citizenship is not decisive. The Workers' Party asked for legal assistance to ensure judicial redress in this case. We haven't got that. That is why we are promoting a loose proposal in the Chamber today, together with the Central Party and the Christian People's Party, asking the government to come back with proposals for the loss of citizenship in the interests of fundamental national interests and for such losses to be decided by the Court of Justice. The Right Party and the Progressive Party propose that they seek urgent judicial proceedings. Of course, the government is welcome to investigate quick tracks. Government parties don't have to ask Stortinget for permission to do this. Of course, if the government wants a quick court hearing and makes suggestions on how this should be done, we will support it. That is why we will be voting in favour of the proposal to speed up court proceedings in the Chamber today. But I would like to point out that it is funny that the Right Party and the Advance Party need us to adopt a directive, so that the government will do something the Workers' Party has asked for, and not received. This case has received a lot of publicity and has been characterized by heated rhetoric. The Labour Party has been accused of running terrorists' cases when we call about the principles and institutions of the rule of law. In this room the heads must be cool and the words carefully weighed. Our decisions are not suffrages. They're the laws of the country. With this I address the proposal of the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and the Christian People's Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e818	The proposal to introduce a legal framework to deprive persons of their Norwegian citizenship when they have committed criminal offences challenging fundamental national interests is based on the NOU 2015:4 Loss of Norwegian citizenship. The government points to the legislative proposal as a follow-up to the Action Plan against radicalisation and violent extremism of 2014. In the March 2015 public inquiry by the government, it was concluded that loss of citizenship must be regarded as punishment under the Constitution and the European Convention on Human Rights, and that it must therefore be imposed by the courts. It was also recommended that the loss of citizenship be linked to the terrorist regime and to the rules on crimes against the government, genocide, war crimes and crimes against humanity. The investigation included: that there was only a breach of provisions with a 21-year penalty framework that would lead to the loss of citizenship. Part of the mandate for NOU 2015:4 was to illustrate how and to what extent loss of citizenship could contribute to the prevention of terrorism and radicalisation. The conclusions of the assessments made were that the loss of citizenship would restrict access by foreign fighters to Norway, but on the other hand increase the threat to Norwegian interests abroad. A negative consequence is that it becomes more difficult for Norwegian authorities to control the activities of the former foreign national. It was also raised doubts as to whether the loss of citizenship would appear preventative or radicalising. It states that the rules may work against their purpose by adversely affecting cooperation between authorities and Muslim communities. The Centre Party, together with the other parties to the Committee, has concluded that it is correct and necessary to use the loss of citizenship as a response to acts and behaviour which are strongly detrimental to the vital interests of the country. The introduction of a new rule of law into the national law will apply to persons holding double citizenship. The loss of citizenship shall not be used as a reaction form until the person is 18 years old, nor shall it be used in cases where it would be disproportionate to the person himself or to his family. Of course, in matters relating to children, the best interests of the child should be fundamental. The Committee endorses the Government's proposals in these areas. As far as the majority is concerned, the proposal to lower the penalty framework for the loss of citizenship, from 21 years of age, proposed in the public investigation, to 6 years, proposed by the Right Party and the Progressive Party of Government. This is the view of the majority that it is right to strengthen the possibility of depriving people who threaten the security of the state, their Norwegian citizenship. In one area, we are separating layers with the government and with the minority from the Right Party and the Advance Party of the Committee. This applies to the majority’s support for the assessment given that the loss of citizenship is to be judged by the courts. The Government, on the other hand, and the minority of the Committee consider that the loss of citizenship shall be capable of administrative adoption. In recent days, the Committee’s configuration has been subject to media playback and Facebook comments. Yes, so far the debate has gone, that what was a discussion about citizenship, in today's newscasts ended up as a discussion about passports, among other things. at NRK. This makes the debate not exactly elucidating for the ordinary elector, and necessarily not clarifying for the members of the Storting. There is an overall committee which will strengthen the provisions of the Civil Code by introducing new provisions allowing persons to lose their Norwegian citizenship by, as it says, ‘promotion strongly to the detriment of’ the vital interests of the country. We agree that Norway must resort to stronger instruments against terrorist planning, illegal war participation and which pose a threat to its security. What we disagree about is how to treat as strong reactions as loss of citizenship. The majority follow the advice given by the government body itself. I am sure that we are on a safe basis in this conclusion. The members of the government parties from the Right and the Advance Party, among other things, put forward proposals asking the Government ‘to determine whether a system of prompt judicial review can be established on matters relating to the loss of citizenship in the interests of fundamental national interests’. The central party believes that it is important that matters relating to the protection of national security are dealt with effectively and rapidly in the judiciary. I assume that this is a matter that the government is following up, regardless of decisions in the Storting, when we now decide the law on how to deal with cases of loss of citizenship. Nevertheless: I record that you need to ask for an investigation of a rapid court. It shall, of course, contribute to the broad majority of the Centre, if that is what government parties need in order to launch such an investigation.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1103	Now we're going to deal with two propositions, Prop. 148 L for 2016-2017 and Prop 33 L for 2017-2018. There is a 144-page setting - there is a lot of reading. In its proposal, the Ministry proposes that Norway follow the same model as many other countries in Europe, by carrying out the property survey as a professional private service. According to the proposal, the reform will contribute to increased competition, faster and more efficient services for users and the development of a new consultancy for property services. There shall be free pricing on measurement transactions instead of municipal fees that are currently charged, and the payment will reflect more closely the actual costs of performing the specific service, according to the proposition. The Government also proposes that the responsible surveyor with authorisation be responsible for the survey and that it be carried out in accordance with the law and regulations of good land surveyors. Furthermore, it is proposed that the training requirement should be at the bachelor's level, three years, and that there should be a period of practice of at least two years. In order to maintain the authorisation, surveyors must take a certain number of hours of training per year and refer to, for example: such an arrangement as the real estate agents have. The Ministry believes that flexible initial authorisation solutions need to be provided, and a large proportion of municipal practitioners also expect to have the necessary real skills. The Ministry further proposes that these practitioners be authorised in line with new practitioners even if they do not meet the requirements of formal competence. The Committee points out that a majority of the hearing bodies are positive about the introduction of formal competence requirements. The Committee draws attention, inter alia, to: NITO’s consultation call, which states that the time is ripe to formalise the commitment to competence made in the context of the implementation of the Matrikkel law, essentially in the direction of the municipalities. The majority do not support the government’s proposal on free pricing for measuring operations. The majority of the Committee, i.e. the Labour Party, the Centre Party, the SV and the Christian People's Party, believe that the matriculation is a clear authority task which should be organised as a public administration task. The majority indicate that a large majority of the hearing bodies argue for the continuation of the day’s model, where municipalities are responsible for both the property survey and the property registration, and that this is closely linked. The majority support these assessments and believe that municipalities must be responsible for the recording of property information together with the responsibility for the property survey. On the basis of this, the majority will not support the government's proposals. The Committee points out that the government's proposal for a distinction between measurement such as the provision of services and the provision of food as a management task has an impact on the complaint mechanism. The majority – again the Workers' Party, the Centre Party, SV and the Christian People's Party – indicate that they are opposed to the government's proposal for such a distinction, so that the administrative appeal system is maintained as it is today. The Committee supports the other government proposals, which do not address the organisational issue. The majority do not support the government’s proposals related to the organisational issue and otherwise refer to the majority’s overall legislative proposals. Thus, the majority do not support the government’s proposal to privatize surveyors’ tasks, nor does it support the distinction between surveying and recording. The majority believe that this is an important overall task, which is still to be carried out in an overall framework. Then a small correction has to be made. If we don't have to deal with this a third time, and we have to try to avoid that. I'll try to get this fairly understandable. We have been made aware of the fact that in the proposal for a law in the committee's recommendation there is a so-called orthographic error, which then needs to be corrected: the proposal for the last sentence of the fourth paragraph of Section 26 states the 'central matricle governing authority' – with an a in the end. Norwegian law shall be ‘central matricle steering power’ — without a final a. This has no material or material significance for the rest, so I'm just suggesting that the decision be in line with this.

This would have contributed to more value creation by enabling one and the same surveyor company to have assisted the developers throughout the project period and to provide faster clarifications and better progress in the projects. ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1151	In this proposition, the Government proposes that Norway follow the same model as in many other countries in Europe, by carrying out property surveys as a professional private service. This will contribute to increased competition, faster and more efficient services for users and the development of a new consultancy industry. The idea was free pricing for measuring stores instead of municipal fees as today. The payment will then reflect more closely the actual costs. The proposal facilitates the more flexible organisation of both municipal and private surveyors who can provide such services on equal terms. As a result, real estate measurement will no longer be a statutory task for the municipalities. Municipalities may, as long as they wish, offer measurement. Municipalities that choose to maintain their surveyor’s offer will be free to provide their services in other municipalities. A responsible surveyor with authorisation shall be responsible for carrying out the survey in accordance with laws, regulations and administrative provisions and in accordance with good surveyor practice. He does not have to do all the tasks himself, but is responsible. The surveyor shall take care of the interests of all parties to the surveying business. The authorisation will oblige the responsible surveyor to act neutrally and the surveyor may lose the authorisation if he does not. The Government proposes that the training requirement be of three years' duration and of a minimum period of two years' experience. Retraining is also required. When measuring business is no longer to be a statutory task for the municipalities, the task is proposed to be transferred to the Staten Kartverk, which is currently responsible for the management and organisation of the matrix. Municipalities who so desire shall be able to retain property information in the matrix when they have sufficient capacity and professional competence. Municipalities shall continue to be responsible for keeping information on buildings and addresses in the matrix. Fees for data recording shall follow common national rates in accordance with fees rules laid down in the Central Regulation. Menon Economics AS, on behalf of the Ministry, has carried out a socio-economic analysis of the consultation proposal. In the report, Menon expects the measure to be socially viable. Today's system is not necessarily neutral, as the municipality itself is often party to the cases as the landowner. The amendment may allow users to obtain prices from several suppliers, both private and public, and to choose a supplier based on price, delivery time and competence. Price formation will thus be fairer and a more realistic price-to-real cost relationship will be established, i.e. a very general way of thinking, as for most other goods and services. There are huge price differences across municipalities today. Those who are most affected by this are those who are always most affected, namely those who have the least. At the other end, owners of larger land, such as farmers in agriculture, forestry and cabin development, are affected. This is both unfortunate and hostile to districts, and it will defuse and delay the development of land. There are also large differences between treatment periods across municipal borders. For several municipalities, the average waiting time is over a hundred days longer than in neighbouring municipalities. This is unfortunate for the development of property and value creation in general. I note that the proposition contains a number of changes supported by an overall committee, with the exception of the issue of property surveying being a private service and with a new form of organisation. The Progress Party also supports this, as we believe that overall it will provide us with a better and more flexible offer for the benefit of all parties. We know that competition works, and we know that interaction between public and private works.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1248	The matter today is a follow-up of Stortinget’s request decision to the government in March 2016. Land measurement is both about calculating map coordinates and about being able to put two lines below the answer. But it's also about jus. The parties may have a totally different view of where a boundary is going and may oppose each other in an incompatible way. It cannot solve the calculation of map coordinates, but neither can the municipality do as administrative body. Disputes about property must be settled by the parties themselves or with the assistance of the court. The municipality shall also be careful to provide legal advice to parties in a conflict of private law. In Norway, property surveying has been a responsibility of the municipalities. Others have solved this differently. Several have it as a professional service, e.g. magazines in Denmark. Other countries, such as Sweden, have organised the task more in the direction of legal land change, primarily as a state responsibility. The government therefore proposes to organise the property survey as a professional service also in Norway. The proposal will contribute to the development of a new sector of property. It will make it easier, faster and more efficient to carry out property surveys and development projects. At the same time, the Government proposes to transfer responsibility for the management of property information to the State at the Swedish Cartel Office. This part of the proposal is a direct consequence of the main proposal for a new organisation of property surveys. When local authorities are no longer required to have a legal liability for property measurement, they should also have no legal liability for property registration. The Government proposes the introduction of an authorisation system for property surveyors to ensure the competence and thereby improve the quality of the matrix. The Ministry will now immediately begin to develop supplementary provisions for the authorisation system in close dialogue with municipalities and surveyors. ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1490	The main purpose of this proposal is to help the police to obtain an overview of non-residents living in the individual police districts. Police shall also receive such information as regards residents who have leave from reception or from the care centre. Information may be required when necessary for police tasks under the Foreign Affairs Act or tasks related to police purposes. The information may include, inter alia: be obtained when necessary in the context of the police’s work on foreign controls. Knowledge of the residents of different reception and care centres will put police districts in a significantly better position to prevent and prevent the presence of, for example: criminal acts and other violations of public order and security. Kripos emphasises that today’s legal situation creates uncertainty and limits the police’s ability to perform their duties effectively when such information is relevant. ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1505	The legislative proposal we are examining is that, upon written request, the police should be provided with information on the name, date of birth and nationality of current and former residents of asylum centres and care centres for single minor asylum seekers. The aim of the proposal is to help the police to obtain an overview of foreign residents in each police district, so that police can carry out their functions more easily as a control body in the foreign field, promote the work of security and preparedness, and facilitate the prevention and investigation of criminal offences. It is important for the police to have effective access to information on who lives or has lived in asylum recipients and care centres. The information may contribute to the effective prevention of crime, the fight against crime, the investigation of crime and the establishment of contingency plans. Kripos emphasises that today’s legal situation creates uncertainty and limits the police’s ability to perform their duties effectively when such information is relevant. Information on reception residents will also improve police preparation in their regular contact with reception and may be of mutual benefit to both receptions, residents and police. As with several hearing bodies, the government considers that the same considerations justifying the police’s need for information about residents in reception apply to residents in care centres for single minor asylum seekers. This is a highly vulnerable group, and the information could be central to preventing minors from being subjected to serious crime or being influenced to participate in criminal offences. Some hearing bodies have pointed out that it is not clear that the purpose of the change cannot be achieved by the law of the day. According to Sections 9 to 3 of the People's Register Regulation The fifth paragraph shall provide the police with the information deemed necessary by the public register authorities. However, asylum seekers residing in reception and care centres are not registered in the population register. Fear The children point out that the police have no opportunity to collect similar information about children from other child protection institutions in order to prevent or detect criminal acts. This is not entirely correct, as children in child welfare institutions have a residence address, which we again find in the population register. In addition to the fact that the police will have access to the name, date of birth and nationality, the proposal states that the police can be given access to information about when they arrived at the reception and when they moved on. To the extent that the reception or UDI has information about it, the police should also be informed of a new residence. Foreigners who have an application for treatment are obliged to inform the police about the change of their place of residence. The proposal states that there must be a justification for the request, even if this justification is not to be provided to the receptions or care centres. Reasons must be given as to the purpose for which the information is to be used and as to the nature of the case. However, I agree that the provision of information on children requires that special vulnerability of children be taken into account. Police must therefore look to the best interests of the child when carrying out assessments of the need. It follows from the Constitution and the Convention on the Rights of the Child that the best interests of the child shall be a fundamental consideration in actions and decisions affecting children. The Government considers that it is not appropriate for the receptions to be granted a review authority, since the receptions do not have the necessary police or legal competence to assess whether information is necessary for the police. The control task has been entrusted to the Data Protection Authority. It must be assumed that most residents are aware that the Norwegian authorities have access to information on their name, date of birth, citizenship and where they live, in the same way that the police have access to this information on other Norwegian citizens and foreigners registered in the population register. As the police district of Oslo points out, otherwise good justification and information for the group for which information is provided could help limit the sense of infringement and ensure the legal certainty of the individual. Children are particularly vulnerable to trafficking, abuse and exploitation, and it is the responsibility of society for children living in Norway. We have a specific responsibility for the care of children's lives, health and development. Therefore, the progress party supports the proposition.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1537	The centre party believes that the purpose of the bill promoted in the proposal is well justified. There is a need for the police to have an overview of non-residents living in the individual police districts, and it is therefore also desirable that the police be able to obtain information about residents in reception. This is otherwise information that the police could have obtained on other nationals registered in Norway through the public register and the postal register but not applicable to those in receipt. The proposal therefore applies to the provision of information about current and former residents in reception and care centres, and also to persons with leave from reception and care centres. It is underlined that the obligation of reception, care centres or UDIs to provide information is limited to informing them of information they have. Refugee service staff shall not be used to collect information that is not already available to them. While the proposal on access to information by the police is well justified by the need to carry out its functions as control body for the foreign field and to promote the work on security and crime prevention, legitimate objections may also be raised. The Ministry says that the police’s use of the authority to obtain information about current and former residents must be balanced between the individual’s need for protection and the needs of society justifying the provision of information. Few people live in reception or care centres that pose some safety risks. Many residents fear the authorities of their own country and have generally seen little confidence in the authorities. It requires good judgement when the information is obtained. We note that the consultation has reacted to the fact that it also opens up information about children living in asylum centres and care centres for lone minors. The Ministry points out that the best and the vulnerability of the child on the one hand indicates that information about the child should not be provided from reception or care centres. On the other hand, the child’s best interests also provide special protection against criminal acts and the police must be able to prevent the child from taking part in criminal acts himself. In a note to the Hearing Opinion of the Association, the Parties stress that human rights are subject to legal requirements, legitimate purposes and justification in cases where there is interference with the right to privacy. The Association of Lawyers considers that the proposal that the police may require information without justification is not sufficiently justified in its proposal. We have therefore asked questions as to whether a justification should be required for the disclosure of information, and that it should be written down and form part of the file in the individual case. The majority of the Committee refers to the need for police to address a written request to the reception in order to obtain information. It also stated that disclosure of information should be necessary in order to carry out police duties. The majority believe that a written request ensures sufficient traceability of the information requested by the police. It is the total, complex part of the information, i.e. how it is compiled, that will determine whether or not you are in conflict with the legislation. That's why, in the opinion of the Center Party, it's good that we get a reprobability. A minority believe that the request for transparency shall be justified in writing and recorded in the Foreign Affairs and Refugees Computer System. Nevertheless, the central party will vote for the vote as it exists on the part of the majority. Consideration of reprobability is taken into account in our view of the requirement for a written request for transparency, but we believe it is important that caution be exercised by the police when requesting access to the personal data of residents at receptions.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1576	I am pleased that this is now the majority of the government's proposals. ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1844	The matter we are dealing with today is a very important matter. It is about how to handle children in a good way, while at the same time maintaining and enforcing Norwegian refugee and asylum policies. Norway has a rough refugee and asylum policy, but it is essential that we ensure that those who are finally rejected and do not stay in Norway are sent out as soon as possible. Today there are many good arrangements for voluntary returns. Asylum seekers with a final rejection of the application are offered an assisted return and a large amount of money if they travel on a voluntary basis. Of course, it is best for all parties that the return is voluntary, but in some cases people with final refusal refuse to leave the country. Some of them have children and, unfortunately, choose to put the children under the extra burden of not cooperating with the authorities to comply with a legally adopted decision. Today, there is really no difference between prisoning adults and children, even though the case law has been correctly tightened. But it is still very correct and important that we get this case, which sets a clear framework for the detention of children, on the table. Of course, there should be stricter criteria for the detention of children than for adults. Even before the new legislation has been adopted — if adopted — the police have begun to act accordingly. Children's families are no longer interned at Trandum, but at a temporary children's and family's home, Haraldvangen. A new permanent boarding school is being planned, and we hope that it will be put in place as soon as possible. The Government's proposal states that in the event of the detention of children, this shall be done with a notice of three days at a time. If the total length of the detention is to exceed six days, it shall be strictly special in order for it to take place. In that case, there must be conditions that are not due to the work of the police but that the person himself is responsible for the fact that the shipment has not taken place. Addressing current legislation is a major improvement in the legal certainty of children and the best interests of the child. There is a proposal that we should set a maximum time of 24 hours and a maximum of three days if there are special conditions. In practice, such a provision, although safe, would mean that the children could be subjected to additional stress, because it is the intrusion of detention that is burdensome for the children. If you don't have children sent out after 72 hours for different reasons, you can risk letting the children out again, getting false hopes, and then the police come and pick them up again. We will avoid shielding the children from the additional loads that may result. That is why we want a higher limit, so that we can have control, with good professional competence and good supervision, and be sure that these people, who are in a difficult situation, are properly cared for. There is also a proposal from the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party and SV that we should use footlinks as an alternative before boarding. Nor do I doubt that the intention of that proposal is good, but I regret to say that this too could be detrimental to the children. It is stated in the comments of these parties that if the conditions of use of the footlink are violated, severe penalties must be imposed. I'll put the best of the child in front of it. So if we first order someone to use footlinks and break the terms, we have to punish the family, with the extra burdens that it will bring on the family, and then we have to internalize in the usual way, with the extra burdens that it would have to lead to. Although the purpose of testing electronic footlinks is first certainly good, I imagine that in many situations it can lead to extra loads – which are not intended – for the children, and that is something we should not use. If you're going to introduce something like this, you have to figure it out properly first. Very well known, there are no countries that use footlinks in foreign affairs in this way, and there are certainly good reasons for this. That is why, in the Progress Party, we primarily support the proposal as it stands, but we support the solutions proposed by the Central Party in a secondary way, and we support secondary proposal No. Six, from the Center Party.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1863	We're going to try again, now that we've come to the right part of the Foreign Affairs Act. Such derogations must be strictly regulated and justified separately. The central party has considered that the government has set up too broad a framework for such detention. However, we are also sceptical about the proposal that has been promoted by the minority, with an absolute ceiling of 72 hours. This may mean that families which for various reasons are not transported on a planned basis may be subjected to several attacks, which would have to be experienced as being very dramatic for the children involved. Then, in our opinion, it would be better to stay at the detention centre for a few more days. The centre party has promoted two own proposals, which I hope to have the House's support here today as subsidiary support. I'm taking up the proposals from the Center Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1928	The left is pleased that the bill seeks to clarify and clarify the rules applicable to the use of coercive funds under the Foreign Affairs Act. The left is therefore pleased that families with children are no longer to be interned on Trandum. Trandum is not a suitable place for children. The government is now working to establish a permanent child and family home at Eidsvoll, and until it is in place, the temporary boarding school at Haraldvangen will be used. In several budget rounds, the Left has contributed to ensuring more resources to strengthen the child-reliance skills of the Police Foreign Unit. By no longer using Trandum, and by establishing a safer and better framework around what is still a dramatic situation, the situation has improved. In addition, we need to look at whether the attacks can also be done in a more gentle way for the children. In Norway, we do not imprison children by criminal acts, either alone or with their families. Correspondingly, this should apply in the case of postings, even when children are interned with their parents. According to Article 3 of the Convention on the Rights of the Child, incorporated in Norwegian law and in the Constitution § 104, the best interests of the child should be a fundamental consideration in all matters relating to children. Furthermore, Article 37b of the Convention on the Rights of the Child states that children shall be deprived of liberty only as a last resort and for the shortest possible time. This implies that other alternatives must have been tested first. In most cases, incarceration for 24 hours is sufficient to carry out a return, and this should therefore also be the clear rule. However, we recognise that there may be conditions that require detention for up to 72 hours. Such an approach would be a strong intensification of the practice that has taken place in recent years. It also implies that forced return of families with children needs to be planned and implemented in a different way than for adults. Force returns are actually – and should be – the last resort. The left is open to consider several alternatives to the internment of children. We are not convinced that footlinks are a good solution for the children. Experience with the use of footlinks as an alternative to ordinary housing is not directly transferable by being in a forced situation. In such a situation, it is important that the whole family receive the necessary professional follow-up, and it is not obvious that you will get this if you stay outside a reception. However, we are open to further investigation and hope that other possibilities will also be considered. More work should be done to obtain voluntary return of families with children. Voluntary returns make it easier to get started with the new life after return. The framework for the detention of children will be the same for children who are interned with their parents and for children who are interned on their own. However, children who are interned on their own are in an extra vulnerable situation, without anyone relying on them. For these children, it is also important that we look at alternatives to incarceration. We therefore support the proposal to look at real alternatives to internment for these children. Finally, on our vote: we support the proposal for a decision by an overall committee that families with children should only be housed in their own children's and families' centres, which are specially organised for minors. In addition, we support minority proposals Nos. One, two, five, six.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1945	Children on the run are particularly vulnerable and completely innocent in their situation. The matter we are dealing with today deals with several aspects, but this is essentially about children's legal certainty. When it comes to the step of forcing a family to return, it is absolutely essential that this be done in a way that is as gentle as possible for the children. Detention is one of the most intrusive states that can impose on an individual. It is therefore essential that the legislation be designed in a way that does not conflict with fundamental human rights. Last year the state was sentenced to the law for, among other things, the duration of the stay of a family of children on Trandum. The Court found that the detention in the present case was contrary to the law. In addition, it broke with the Constitution, the European Convention on Human Rights and the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child. It's totally unsustainable. We must therefore ensure that the law is good within the Constitution and our obligations under international law. In its opinion on the matter, the National Institute for Human Rights has stated to the Committee that neither the Constitution, the European Convention on Human Rights or the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child imposes an absolute ban on the detention of children, but the conditions are strict and the State's scope for action is harsh. The European Court of Human Rights practice shows that the key elements of the assessment of the detention of children are the physical conditions of the detention centre, the age of the child and the duration of the detention. The Convention on the Rights of the Child obliges Norway to ensure that detention is only a last resort and for the shortest possible period of time. In order to ensure that we are good within our obligations under international law, the Christian People's Party, the Labour Party and the Socialist Left have proposed that the main rule of detention for children should be set at 24 hours, but that it may exceptionally be extended to 72 hours if someone in the family gets sick, or if there is new information on the matter. Unfortunately, there is no majority for this, because the Central Party has chosen to give support to the Right and the Advance Party, and I must say that is disappointing. But what is positive is that a Joint Committee supports the proposal for clear requirements for the physical conditions of the detention centre and, not least, the content and offer of the children and families. The Christian People's Party has been committed to ensuring that children cannot be interned at Trandum, but in their own children's and family's homes. This is important in view of the criticism that the Civil Ombudsman has brought to the family unit at Trandum. I am therefore pleased that this is now being adopted. But, as I said, the Convention on the Rights of the Child sets clear limits to the fact that the incarceration of children is only one of the last resorts. Therefore, the option of repatriation detention must be considered before we go to the serious step of invading children. Today, however, there are no alternatives to incarceration. The Christian People's Party therefore believes that electronic footlinks for parents in forced return should be introduced as an alternative to detention. The background is that you can get a better process for your children when they're going out of the country. It will give them the opportunity to say good-bye to their classmates and friends and ensure better treatment of their children by forced repatriation. Neither electronic footlink nor internment are optimal solutions. The Christian People's Party therefore believes that more effort must be made to work on voluntary returns, and those who do not have the basis for staying in Norway, families who have been refused, must be helped to understand the rejection and why they are not allowed to stay. It is therefore necessary to strengthen the provision of information at reception, since this will mean that there will be fewer coercion and that it will prevent children from being interned. That is why I would like to point out the proposals that the rapporteur has made on behalf of SV, the Workers' Party and the Christian People's Party.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e1960	The government has put forward a proposal for a comprehensive revision of the rules on coercive measures of the Foreign Affairs Act, i.e. on investigation, seizure, reporting and specific place of residence, and on seizure and detention. The relevant rules are important for the implementation of Norwegian immigration policy. The rules are mainly justified either by the need to clarify the identity of a foreigner or by the need to make a forced return of someone subject to a foreign obligation. I believe that the bill makes the legislation much clearer than it is today. These changes will thus enhance the legal certainty of those to whom the coercive measures are addressed. These changes will also help to make working life easier for professionals — the police, lawyers and courts. I am therefore very pleased that the main parts of the proposals have been well received in the hearing and that an overall committee has supported most of the proposals. That part of the bill that has created the most discussion concerns access to the detention of children. In order to implement Norwegian immigration policy, it is very important that the legislation, including in the case of families with children, contains sufficient instruments to achieve rapid transport after refusal for those who do not use organised assisted return schemes. If an effective return process is not enforced, cases of families living in Norway occur long after rejection. This in turn leads to individual cases of aggravating return of families with children who have established strong links before transporting actually takes place. This is a situation where there is broad political agreement that it is important to avoid. Experience with families with children who do not send is not special for Norway either. That is why we also find legislation and the possibility of apprehending and incarceration of families with children in the countries we usually compare with, as well as in the EU Return Directive. Countries that have tried to cut the use of detention against families with children have experienced difficulties in obtaining return. This applies, inter alia, to: Belgium and the Netherlands, which have therefore reintroduced the use of detention. The bill proposes a separate paragraph, § 106c, which ensures the legal certainty of children in matters of detention in a better way than today. I note that this is also stated by the Norwegian National Institute for Human Rights. The provision in question provides, inter alia, for: a clear requirement to be made clear in the knowledge of detention how the best interests of the child and the options for alternative measures have been assessed. In addition, entirely new time limits have been proposed, which are significantly shorter than today’s opportunities for the detention of families with children for two weeks at a time. I note from the committee position that a minority in the Committee has made proposals for a time limit of 24 and 72 hours respectively for the detention of families with children. I believe that the proposition explains in detail why such a time limit – especially if it should be as scarce as 72 hours – is unfortunate. The deadline will set a clear target for those entering to oppose return, and may also have the consequence that in some cases families will have to be released from detention after three days in order to be re-triggered, for example. Two days later. If we assume that the attack itself will often be the most uplifting thing for the children, it's hard to see that this can be for the child's best. I believe that such a time limit as the minority suggests, is also completely unnecessary after we have now established our own family unit with a much more civil and family-friendly character than was the case with Trandum. I stress that there are neither high barbed wire fences nor flying noise around the new unit, and families are not locked in the rooms. I believe it is a safe and good framework that, together with the legislative proposal, represents a clear improvement on the situation of families with children who have to be forced to return in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e2087	As rapporteur, I would like to thank the Committee for its thorough and important work on this representative proposal from the SV and the Christian People's Party. The intention of the proposal is to ask the government to make proposals as soon as possible for amendments to the Foreign Affairs Act which allow for a response to violations of the Foreign Affairs Act with non-refoulement and non-refoulement reactions. Some reason why the proposers wish to promote the proposal is that the number of expulsions due to violations of the Foreign Affairs Act has increased sharply in recent years. According to statistics from the Directorate of Foreign Affairs, 2,645 persons were expelled in 2017 due to violations of the Foreign Affairs Act, 3,929 in 2016 and 3,881 in 2015. In total, the number was less than 1000 per year until 2008 and the 20th century was well below 500 per year. There is an overall committee which points to the fact that decisions on expulsion and entry bans are not formally punishable but an administrative response established by the foreign authorities. Furthermore, it is important to stress that Section 108 of the International Criminal Code provides for fines and/or imprisonment to be imposed for up to six months in cases of intentional or negligent violation of the Foreign Affairs Act. It is the Riksadvokat who gives leadership on how the prosecution authority should give priority to criminal prosecution in cases of violations of the Foreign Affairs Act. Expulsion is assessed regardless of whether or not the foreigner is prosecuted. It is also important to stress that Section 70 of the Foreign Affairs Act should ensure proportionality and, in some cases, reduce the entry ban somewhat, for example: from five to two years. Furthermore, UNE and UDI have special knowledge and experience in handling cases and provide the best possible assessment in cases of non-residents’ violation. Today, a foreigner cannot be expelled if, taking into account the seriousness of the relationship and the connection of the foreigner to the rich, it would be disproportionate to the foreigner himself or to the immediate family members. In such cases involving children, the best interests of the child shall be considered as fundamental considerations. It is important to understand that although the objective conditions for expulsion are met, expulsion cannot be decided if the decision is a disproportionate measure to the foreigner himself or to his immediate family members. The requirement of proportionality is not only enshrined in the Foreign Affairs Act, but also in our international obligations. It is crucial whether the grounds for expulsion, i.e.: the seriousness of the infringement is proportionate to the negative effects it has on family and private life. This is carefully assessed before a decision is taken and it is also reflected in whether the expulsion is permanent or limited in time. It is certainly right, even in accordance with the practice of the European Court of Human Rights, that foreign nationals may be expelled even if they have children in Norway and that this issue falls within the discretion of each State. With that I have explained the matter, and we in the Progress Party join the Committee's recommendation.

It is about justice with regard to the decisions to be taken, which shall apply if you or yours fulfil the conditions laid down in the Foreign Affairs Act. Then there must be penalties when someone has deliberately failed to provide correct information. The representatives who have brought this case are concerned with the cases in which children are affected by penalties. And then we're going to have an assessment of whether we can add new forms of reaction. Today, it is true that if there is a breach of the Foreign Affairs Act and it is found that with the current sanctions there are disproportionate measures against foreigners themselves or their immediate family members, there is no real consequence. Here you can actually add the necessary penalties as a day-to-day fine to be clear that the Foreign Affairs Act is based on trust, while considering supplementing with sanctions to ensure that the best interests of children are better than what we do today.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e2131	The centre party believes that it should continue to be possible to react with expulsion and entry bans in the event of a serious breach of the Foreign Affairs Act. Expulsion and entry bans are not formally punishable, but an administrative response established by the foreign authorities. The Foreign Affairs Act states that fines and/or prison sentences can be imposed on cases involving infringements of the Foreign Affairs Act, but this presupposes that the foreign authorities report the matter to the police and that the situation is judged in the courts. Proposals are of the opinion that proposals should be made to amend the Foreign Affairs Act so that the administrative responses may be different from the ban on expulsion and entry. The proposal points out that such reactions may be fines and/or period of quarantine for permanent residence permits and citizenship. These are reaction forms that affect the person who has committed the crime, but which affect the person’s family members to a lesser extent. In the Central Party, we have noticed that the UDI has made a similar proposal, but has not won the hearing of the Council of State. In her letter to the Committee, the Council of State has stated that she believes that the criminal response to serious offences should be left to the prosecution authorities. Expulsion and prohibition of entry are not to be regarded as punishment but as administrative forms of reaction. I think the proposers from the Christian People's Party and the SV have a good argument when they point out that many public authorities have the right to charge a fee in the event of a breach of legislation within their territory, without contradicting the fact that it is also possible to punish the same situation with fines and prisons. The comments also refer to Circular RS 2010-02, which states that, as a general rule, UDI should not report violations of the Foreign Affairs Act to the police if there are grounds for expulsion. In other words, the starting point is that there is an administrative response that is to be used primarily in response to violations of the Foreign Affairs Act, not punishment. The centre party does not wish to weaken the possibility of expulsion and entry bans in the event of a serious breach of the Foreign Affairs Act, but sees the need for the foreign authorities to have a broader set of responses when specific circumstances so warrant, such as the best interests of children. Together with the members of the Workers' Party, SV and the Christian People's Party, we promote a proposal asking the government to investigate and promote any proposals for changes to the Foreign Affairs Act that allow the use of expulsion and entry bans to be complemented by a broader set of reaction forms, such as administrative response forms, in the event of a breach of the Foreign Affairs Act. I must also say that it is difficult to see the principle of some of the reactions of the Right and the Advance Party on this proposal. They believe that the proposal will be resource-intensive to implement and address a number of challenges, including: the determination of the amount of the fees and the inability to pay for those charged. We know that the UDI has previously addressed similar arrangements to those that the majority will now have investigated. It should therefore not be so problematic for an overall increase to ask the government to come back with an investigation and any proposals to supplement the use of expulsion and entry bans for violations of the Foreign Affairs Act. This is particularly necessary in the interests of children. We've seen that in several cases lately.

But then we have to target it, not criminalise legitimate businesses and make it more difficult for Norwegian businesses by restricting their opportunities. Today, we are debating several proposals that are all about the same, ensuring a clean working life. The Right Party, the Advance Party and the Left believe that a well-regulated and decent working life is the foundation of a well-functioning society. The government's objective is a safe and flexible, low-free and high-employment labour market. This is essential to ensure the sustainability of the welfare society. The main rule in Norwegian working life is regular employment. Renting and temporary posts shall be a supplement. There are major challenges in some industries, and the government is very serious about the conditions that have been identified, among other things. in the construction industry, especially in the Oslo area. The government has put in place several measures to combat unprofessional actors. Seven a-krim centres have been established and closer cross-border cooperation has been established, with similar supervision in Lithuania, Bulgaria and Poland. The Prime Minister has also taken initiatives vis-à-vis the EU and has invited a joint fight against labour crime. The Ministry sent out three proposals for consultation in June 2017. It was proposed to define fixed-term employment, to provide a new basis for temporary employment in recruitment agencies and to draw up a proposal for a quota for the number of leased-in enterprises. These recordings are under way in the ministry, and it is well known that the government will make proposals for the Storting during the spring session. It is, of course, quite legitimate to make proposals in order to make impatience manifest. But when the former government partners, the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and the SV do not even manage to merge on one solution, it seems more like a political game than the actual will for political solutions. The NHO and the LO have also been in dialogue, but it has been difficult to find a solution that the parties can agree on. This case has many aspects, and several of them may have unintended consequences. A ban would have been very unfortunate for our business, and we believe that it could also have led to a more unsafe working life in the form of more lay-offs and redundancies. Norwegian working life must be flexible so that one can take the tops, while one can be in competition when new jobs are published. On the other hand, it is important to recognise that some groups face challenges in entering the labour market. The government will therefore lower the threshold into the labour market and have better cooperation between employers, Nav, the manning industry, management companies and other actors. The opening of temporary employment and the hiring of labour are strictly regulated by the Labour Environment Act. When the government introduced in 2015 a general access to temporary employment, it also tightened the legislation. The introduction of the agency directive in 2013 helped to strengthen the fight against social dumping and provides for equivalent pay and working conditions in Norway and Europe. Equal treatment is a fundamental principle, but we also recognise that violations of these provisions are being committed in parts of the world of work. This is by no means acceptable, and there is a relationship that one has to deal with and hit hard. Those who want the manning industry to life are talking about renting as a challenge, but are less concerned with the difference between subcontracting and renting. The question is: do any tools work? In a debate we can always play on emotions and rough examples, but it also deserves to be based on facts. The majority of Norwegian working lives are permanently employed. Only 8 pst. is in a temporary position and Fafo estimates that 1,5-2 pst. of all annual works are leased in. In construction, there are 7 pst. and up to 15 pst. in the Oslo area, but it cannot generally be said to fill the whole area. Action must be based on facts. The government has ordered an inquiry into different forms of employment relations that will be submitted during 2018. Here one has also taken in the construction industry. There will also be an evaluation of the introduction of general access to temporary employment. The Labour Party, the Centre Party and the SV write in their comments that ‘each responsible employer tries to adapt the amount of work of the enterprise to its crew’. I have a hard time believing that they can mean that. If we are to have Norwegian jobs where more jobs are created, companies must have the opportunity to adapt the manning to the amount of work, and not the other way around. We need companies that take employment risks, not companies that preserve. Then, as responsible politicians, we must use instruments that punish the unprofessional, while at the same time not tightening our legs under the serious.
