ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e2431	The volume of rentals in the construction industry and industry has increased significantly since the millennium. In particular, after the EEA enlargement in 2004, the share of contracted labour from Eastern Europe has increased sharply in many sectors. This has given employers greater flexibility to meet peaks and bottoms of production. It has lowered employers' costs, where they can again rely more on the output of hired labour when needed than on permanent employment. At the same time, developments have highlighted many and significant disadvantages, both through the consultation organised by the Committee and through press releases in recent months. The Christian People's Party therefore believes that the social adverse effects of today's level of hired labour require vigorous countermeasures by the authorities. Such an action could have been a ban on rentals, for which the Labour Environment Act opens up, and for which the Workers' Party, SV and Red have made various proposals. However, the Christian People's Party believes that these proposals will have a negative impact on value creation. They will make it very difficult for businesses to meet peaks and bottoms in production. For competitive and cyclical industries, such as the shipbuilding and fishing industries, the result would probably have been the deflagration of production and job losses. Changes in the Working Environment Act have a major impact on Norwegian business. The Christian People's Party therefore believes that it is important to get a government-prepared case, rather than giving support to representatives' proposals that have not been investigated in the same way, with the opportunity for interested parties to comment. That is the main reason why we are today choosing to vote against all the proposals that the minority has promoted in the Inner East. 131 S and Innst. 132 L, with the exception of proposal No 6 in Inn. 131 S, which we will support. The government has announced a bill that addresses the issues raised by the Storting today. We in the Christian People's Party will make it clear that in proposal No. 6 is a clear order for the government when the proposition is to be made. Thus, through a thorough investigation in the ministry, where the various issues and proposals for decisions are put to the consultation, we ensure that these complex issues, which directly address how large parts of Norwegian business organise the need for labour against volume of work, are sufficiently addressed before the Stortinget takes binding legislative decisions. The main issue of the Christian People's Party on the use of rentals and temporary employment is as follows: First of all, we want a clearer definition of fixed-term employment. Here we are pleased to note that the Council of State has also sent out clear signals that this is becoming a central point in the announced proposition. Permanent employment should mean that employment is ongoing and unlimited and that the rules of the Act on termination of employment apply. It should also be emphasised that fixed-term employment without pay between assignments does not meet the statutory requirements for permanent employment. Secondly, we want the government to consider proposing an obligation for the employer to provide predictability of work and income through a work plan. We believe that the Labour Environment Act must clarify that the employer has an obligation to pay when the employee is required to remain at his disposal. When the government promotes the billing of the Storting, we also expect an assessment to be made as to whether conditions should be laid down for the average working time of its employees in the preparation of the working plan. This is one of the issues that we will carefully assess when the Storting is going to deal with this issue, in the light of different ways of organising work within different industries. Thirdly, we believe that the government has to consider proposing that access to extended use of fixed-term rentals, as defined in the Working Environment Act § 14-12, second paragraph, be limited, so that only enterprises bound by a country-wide collective agreement with a trade union with a right of cancellation can enter into written agreements on fixed-term rentals. Fourthly, we ask the government to consider proposing that the Labour Inspectorate should be allowed to supervise the rental and sanctioning of illegal rentals. Of course, by the term ‘renting’ we mean both hiring out and hiring out labour. On this basis, in the Christian People's Party, we will vote in favour of the Workers' Party's proposal asking the government to consider introducing the four amendments I have mentioned. At the same time, with our support for the Workers' Party's proposal, we send a clear signal to the government about what will become the Christian People's Party's approach when the matter comes from the government to the Stortinget. Finally, I would like to give a brief reason why the Christian People's Party does not support minority motion No. 1 – proposals for I and II – from the Labour Party and the Socialist Left Party, in Innst. 132 L. Like the majority of the Committee, we believe it is too early to consider making cuts in the overall access to temporary employment, in view of the fact that more research projects are still ongoing and are being completed for the autumn, which could help to shed light on more aspects of issues related to temporary employment that may be of importance. When the results of the investigations are available, the Christian People's Party will conclude on whether or not to continue general access to temporary employment, as formulated in Section 14-9 of the First paragraph of point (f) of the Working Environment Act.

In addition, the impression is that the principle of permanent employment is at stake, by opening up a modest access to rental and temporary employment. But this is not true. None of the claims are correct. Of course, we must fight against the crime of working life, we all agree on that. Useric actors and labour-life crime undermine Norwegian working life and Norwegian workplaces. Work life must and must be safe, flexible and family-friendly for all workers. This is precisely why the fight against crime at work has escalated in recent years. Seven new centres have been set up to combat crime at work, which has already produced good results. In 2016, 3,200 businesses were controlled, twice as many as in the previous year, and 254 criminal networks were revealed. And more controls are coming. It's going to be a further tightening. Service centres are also established for foreign workers to ensure that they receive good information about their rights and obligations.

The Labour Inspectorate has been strengthened with more resources, but they have also been given the opportunity to penalise enterprises that do not ensure that employees have HMS cards. Several service centres have also been established to ensure that foreign workers receive good information about their rights and obligations. Developments in the labour market are now very closely monitored. This is an area that is demanding, where we see criminal networks adapt and renew as new measures are introduced. This is therefore a continuous work in which the government, together with the social partners, cleans up unclear legislation and develops new regulations. When ID cards are now introduced to prevent misuse of ID documents and when the People's Register is renewed, the proper registration of enterprises and persons in public registers has been strengthened. From 1 January 2017, the public acquisition code was tightened up. More stringent requirements are now being imposed on orders and suppliers. In addition, all enterprises relevant to it have been required to have apprentices, i.e. one apprentice per job they do for the country. To prevent foreign criminals from escaping, there is now closer cooperation across borders. The Labour Inspectorate has inter alia: cooperation with equivalent supervision in a number of countries, including Latvia, Bulgaria and Poland. The government’s strategy against labour crime was presented in the autumn of 2015 and reviewed every six months with the parties. The Prime Minister has taken initiatives towards the EU, inviting her to join in the fight against crime in the labour market. The EU itself has introduced 20 principles for new labour policies in its social pillars. We are aware that it can quickly be seen as self-crust when we frame up everything that has been done. The point is that whoever claims that the government does not take the crime of working life seriously is very wrong. I will dare to claim that more has happened the four years we have been sitting with power, than in the period before that. But that doesn't mean that the job is done. We're going to clean up, we're going to pull away the carpet during the foot party that organized crime has got in the labour market in Norway – but the opposition's proposal is not proper medicine.

It's proposed no. 1 and 2, from Jon Engen-Helgheim on behalf of Right and Advance Party proposal No 3, by Karin Andersen on behalf of the Socialist Left Party proposal No 4, by Siri Gåsemyr Staalesen on behalf of the Labour Party, the Center Party and the Christian People's Party proposal No 5, from Audun Lysbakken on behalf of the Labour Party, the Central Party, the Socialist Left Party, the Christian People's Party, the Environmental Party of the Green Party and the Red Party Proposal No. 3, from the Socialist Left Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘In Law No 10 June 2005 51 concerning Norwegian citizenship is amended as follows: In Chapter 5, Section 26(a) is added as follows: § 26 a Loss of citizenship in criminal matters The person who has Norwegian and other citizenships and has demonstrated a serious adverse effect on Norway’s vital interests may, by judgment, lose his Norwegian citizenship if he is penalised by a provision of the penal law, Chapters 16, 17 or 18, which may lead to a prison sentence for 21 years or more. Infringement of Section 127 of the Penal Code for having entered into an association with anyone to commit offences as referred to in Section 123 of the Criminal Code does not, however, justify the loss of Norwegian citizenship under the paragraph here. [[State secret disclosure]] No one shall lose Norwegian citizenship after the paragraph here for acts committed before the age of 18. Norwegian citizenship may not be lost under this paragraph if, taking into account the seriousness of the relationship and the nationality's connection with the State, it would be disproportionate to the person or members of the family closest to him or her. In matters relating to children, the best interests of the child shall be fundamental.’» The Greens and Reds have already given their support to the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e3156	Proposal No. 1, from the Right and the Front, will now be taken up for voting. The proposal reads as follows: ‘I In Law No 10 June 2005 51 on Norwegian citizenship is amended as follows: In Chapter 5, Section 26(a) is added as follows: § 26 a Loss of citizenship in criminal matters The person who has Norwegian and other citizenships and has demonstrated a serious adverse effect on Norway’s vital interests may, by judgment, lose his Norwegian citizenship if he is penalised by a provision of the penal law, Chapters 16, 17 or 18, which may result in a prison sentence for six years or more. Infringement of Section 127 of the Penal Code for having entered into an association with anyone to commit offences as referred to in Section 123 of the Criminal Code does not, however, justify the loss of Norwegian citizenship under the paragraph here. [[State secret disclosure]] No one shall lose Norwegian citizenship after the paragraph here for acts committed before the age of 18. Norwegian citizenship may not be lost under this paragraph if, taking into account the seriousness of the relationship and the nationality's connection with the State, it would be disproportionate to the person or members of the family closest to him or her. In matters relating to children, the best interests of the child shall be fundamental. In Chapter 5, Section 26(b) is added as follows: § 26 b Loss of citizenship for fundamental national interests Anyone who has Norwegian and other citizenship and has behaved strongly to the detriment of Norway's vital interests may lose his Norwegian citizenship for fundamental national interests. No one shall lose Norwegian citizenship after the paragraph here for acts committed before the age of 18. Norwegian citizenship may not be lost under this paragraph if, taking into account the seriousness of the relationship and the nationality's connection with the State, it would be disproportionate to the person or members of the family closest to him or her. In matters relating to children, the best interests of the child shall be fundamental. Between Chapter 6 the title and Section 27 the following title is inserted: I. Administrative proceedings Paragraph 27, first, second and third paragraphs, is replaced by the following: The application for Norwegian citizenship is submitted to the police or Norwegian foreign station preparing the case. The Directorate for Foreign Affairs shall decide on the application. Decisions to reject the application pursuant to the second paragraph of Section 7 shall be taken by the Ministry. Notification of Norwegian citizenship is submitted to the police preparing the case. The Foreign Affairs Directorate decides whether or not to accept the notification. Decisions on withdrawal of citizenship are taken by the Directorate for Foreign Affairs. Decisions on the loss of citizenship under Section 26(b) are taken by the Ministry. Decisions referred to in the first and second subparagraphs may be appealed against to the Department of Foreign Affairs. Decisions under the second paragraph of Section 7 and Section 26(b) may not be appealed. If an action is brought against the validity of the decision of the Ministry under the second paragraph of Section 7 or Section 26(b), the State bears all the costs of the case. The King of the State Council is the Board of Appeal in cases where the Ministry has applied its command authority under the second paragraph of Section 28. The first paragraph of Sections 77, 78 and 79 of the Foreign Affairs Act applies mutatis mutandis to proceedings under the law here. Paragraph 27, seventh paragraph, is replaced by the following: In the case referred to in the fourth subparagraph, that case shall be governed by the right to free jurisdiction without any appropriate examination. In the case referred to in the sixth subparagraph, the person concerned shall have the right to free conduct without examination. By decision taken pursuant to the second paragraph of Section 7 and to the second paragraph of Article 26(b), the case is governed by the right to free judicial advice without examination of the need, free conduct of proceedings without examination of the need and exemption from legal fees. Chapter 6 the following Section II is added: II. Case-law of the Court of Justice of the European Communities relating to the validity of decisions under the second paragraph of Article 7 and Article 26(b) § 31 a Relationship with the Arbitration Act The law of dispute shall apply where there is no other consequence of the rules given in or in accordance with the law here. § 31 b Time limits for actions The decision must be brought before the Court of First Instance within one month of the date on which the decision is notified to the party. Refresh may be granted in the event of a breach of the time limit for bringing proceedings if: (a) information relating to material facts which were or were not known at the time of the decision is submitted to the Court of Justice and that matter shall be brought before the Court as soon as possible after the information is known; or (b) a binding decision of an international court, or other similar fact, indicates that decisions may have been based on incorrect application of international law. § 31 c Appointment of a special lawyer As a condition for the keeping of evidence of circumstances which may otherwise be kept secret for the sake of national security or relations with a foreign State, see: § 22-1 of the Arbitration Act, the King may provide that the information shall be disclosed only to a specific lawyer appointed for that matter. The special lawyer shall be appointed by the court as soon as possible after a decision as referred to in the first subparagraph has been taken and shall be remunerated by the State in accordance with the rules laid down in the Law of 13 June 1980 No. 35 on free legal aid. This applies even if the case is not the subject of a free trial and without any need-to-be examination being carried out. The same special lawyer shall be appointed at all stages of the case, unless specific reasons warrant otherwise. The king shall lay down detailed rules as to who may be appointed as a special lawyer, including requirements for security clearance. § 31 d The role of the separate lawyer The special lawyer shall be made aware of the information and evidence provided on the basis of his consent under the second paragraph of Section 22-1 of the Arbitration Act and shall take care of the interests of that case in relation to its proceedings. The Court of First Instance determines how the special lawyer should have access to the classified information. The special lawyer shall be notified of any court meetings held in the case and shall have the right to be present throughout the proceedings. The special lawyer shall maintain silence on the content of the information referred to in the first subparagraph, on what appears during the processing of the information and on the part of the judgment of the court in which the information is referred. The obligation of professional secrecy shall also apply after the termination of the mission. The king may lay down detailed rules. § 31 e Processing of information referred to in § 22-1 of the Arbitration Act Information referred to in Section 22-1 of the Arbitration Act shall not include witnesses other than those to which the King consents. That case and its ordinary procedural agent shall not participate in the part of the case in which the information referred to in the first subparagraph is dealt with. Such information shall be made known only to the court and to the special lawyer. If the court finds that the conditions laid down in the first paragraph of Section 22-1 of the Arbitration Act do not exist, the court may, on application by the special lawyer or on its own initiative, decide to give it access to the information. In that case, the King may decide that the information shall not be used as evidence in the case. No person who has had access to the information in question may participate as a judge in the proceedings. § 31 f Communication between the special lawyer, the case in question and his ordinary procedural agent After the specific lawyer has been given access to the information referred to in the first subparagraph of Section 31(c), he may not discuss the matter orally or in writing with that matter or with his procedural agent, or speak in legal proceedings in which that matter is involved or his procedural agent is present. However, the special lawyer may receive written enquiries. The king may prescribe the communication between the special lawyer and the case and his ordinary procedural agent. The king may also give instructions on the individual lawyer’s access to a position on the matter in question at a later stage. § 31 g Other rules on the handling of the case by the court The procedure shall be prioritised and accelerated as far as possible. The court may not be set up with a judge or co-judgment. The first paragraph of § 9-12 of the Arbitration Act and § 29-17 do not apply. § 31 h Judgments of the Court of First Instance The Court of First Instance ruled that the party and the individual lawyer had the opportunity to make their views known. The judgment of the Court of First Instance, pursuant to the third paragraph of Section 31(e), shall be delivered by order of the Court of First Instance. The special lawyer or state may appeal within two weeks. That case is pending and its procedural agent is not entitled to have access to the part of the decision of the case which contains the information referred to in the first paragraph of Section 31c. II In Law 13 June 1980 No. 35 in the case of free legal aid is amended as follows: Paragraph 11, first paragraph, point (b) 1 is replaced by the following: 1. for non-residents who are entitled to free legal aid under the first paragraph of Section 92, the second subparagraph and the first sentence of the third subparagraph, or for those who are entitled to free legal aid under the first or third sentence of Section 27 of the seventh paragraph of the Civil Code. Paragraph 16, first paragraph, point (b) 4 is replaced by the following: 4. for foreigners in cases referred to in the second sentence of the third paragraph of Section 92 of the Foreign Affairs Act and the fourth paragraph of Section 129, second paragraph, or for those who are entitled to free movement under the second or third sentence of the seventh paragraph of Section 27 of the Civil Code. III In Law 17 December 1982 No. 86 on the legal fee is amended as follows: Paragraph 10, first paragraph, new paragraph. 17 is replaced by the following: 17th case under the second and third sentence of the third paragraph of Section 27 of the Civil Code, cf. Law 13 June 1980 No. 35 on freedom to provide legal aid § 16, first paragraph, point 4th@item: inlistbox IV In Law 20 May 2005 No 28 on punishment shall be amended as follows: Paragraph 29(f) is replaced by the following: f) loss of rights, cf. A party may refuse to accept the business when the responsible surveyor or any other person carrying out the business: (a) is a party to the proceedings; (b) are in kinship with a party in the ascending or descending line or in the lateral line as close as the sibling; (c) are or have been married to, caged or tendered in a matrimonial relationship with, are or have been engaged to, or have our foster father, foster mother or foster child of a party; (d) is a court or tribunal for a party to the proceedings or has been such a court or tribunal after the commencement of proceedings. ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e6668	Proposal No. 4, from the Workers' Party and the Socialist Left Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The government is asked to propose amendments to the Working Environment Act defining what permanent employment should include. Minimum requirements must be a specified percentage of the employment with a work plan which also provides for a predictable weekly/monthly working period, and these working conditions must be reflected in the contract of employment and thus constitute the basis for the obligation of pay and employment.» The Greens and Reds have already given their support to the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e6685	Proposal No. 1-3, from the Labour Party and the Socialist Left Party. Proposal No. 1 sounds: ‘The government is asking for a ban on labour hiring limited to either the geographically most vulnerable areas or limited to the most vulnerable industries. As an immediate measure, the Stortinget Government is asking for a ban on labour hiring from non-production establishments on construction sites, in the provinces of Oslo, Akershus, Østfold, Buskerud and Vestfold, cf. [[5]] § 14-12 of the Environmental Protection Act by 1 July 2018.’ Proposal No. 2 sounds: «The Government is asked to ensure that illegal labour hiring entails more severe penalties in the form of infringement fees and, where appropriate, quarantine provisions against rental and rental in the event of repeated infringements. The Labour Inspectorate shall be entrusted with the task of supervising illegal rentals.’;» Proposal No. 3 sounds: ‘The Government asks the Government to file a case for the reintroduction of collective actions against the illegal hiring of labour and to extend the right to hire workers.’ The centre party, the environmental party Dei Green and Red have already supported the proposals.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e6715	Proposal No. 6, from the Workers' Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The decision asks the Government of the notified proposal for proposals for amendments to the Working Environment Act on permanent employment, temporary employment and hiring of temporary staff to consider proposing amendments to ensure that: – a clear definition of fixed-term employment, the firmness of employment means that employment is ongoing and unlimited, and that the rules of the Act on the cessation of employment apply, as well as stressing that fixed-term employment without pay between jobs does not meet the statutory requirements for permanent employment. – an obligation on the part of the employer to provide predictability of work and income through a work plan and to prevent contracts in which employees are permanently employed, with the consequence that they must remain at their disposal all the time and nevertheless not be guaranteed more than a part-time job. – that access to rental is restricted in such a way that only establishments bound by a national collective bargaining agreement with a trade union with a right to set off may enter into a written agreement on fixed-term lease payments and that the establishment and the crew member undertaking shall, upon request of the Labour Inspectorate, provide evidence that the activity is bound by a collective agreement with a trade union with a right to set-aside and that an agreement has been concluded with the trustees. – that the Labour Inspectorate is authorised to supervise the rental and sanctioning of illegal rentals. The proposals must be promoted at a time which allows the Storting to take decisions by the summer of 2018.’» The Central Party, the Socialist Left Party, the Christian People's Party, the Green Party and the Red Party have already supported the proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-03-15.ana.ud361e6879	Proposal No. 2, from the Center Party and the Socialist Left Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘In Law No 17 June 2005 62 on the working environment, working time and job protection, etc. [[work environment law]] are changed as follows: I Paragraphs 14 to 9 are replaced by the following: § 14-9. Permanent and temporary staff shall be employed permanently. [[1]] For the purposes of this Act, fixed employment means that employment is continuous and unlimited, that the rules of the Act on termination of employment apply and that the worker is guaranteed predictability of work in the form of a real scope of employment. [[2]] However, temporary contracts may be concluded: (a) where the work is of a temporary nature; b) for work instead of another or another [[viariat]] (c) for practical work; (d) with participants in labour market measures under the auspices of, or in cooperation with, the State of employment and welfare; or (e) with sportsmen, athletes, judges and other leaders of organised sport. The employer shall, at least once a year, discuss the use of temporary employment in accordance with the provisions of this paragraph with the trustees, including the basis and extent of such employment, as well as the impact on the working environment. The Ministry may lay down detailed rules on temporary employment for practical work and on the types of labour market measures covered by point (d) of the second subparagraph. [[3]] [[4]] National employee organisation may conclude a collective agreement with an employer or an employer association on access to temporary employment within a specified employee group to perform artistic, research or sports work. If the collective bargaining agreement is binding on a majority of employees within a specified working group in the establishment, the employer may, under the same conditions, enter into a temporary contract with other employees who are required to carry out similar work. [[5]] Employees who have been employed for more than one year are entitled to written notice of the date of resignation not later than one month before the date of retirement. This does not, however, apply to participants in labour market measures covered by the third subparagraph, as set out in the third subparagraph. the second subparagraph, point (d). The warning shall be deemed to have been given when the worker has been reached. In the event of failure to comply with the deadline, the employer may not require the worker to leave within one month of the notification. [[6]] Temporary employment contracts shall cease at the end of the agreed period or at the end of the specific work, unless otherwise agreed in writing or provided for in the collective bargaining agreement. During the contractual period, the rules of the law on termination of employment apply. [[7]] Employees who have been in continuous temporary employment for more than four years after point (a) of the second subparagraph or for more than three years after point (b) of the second subparagraph shall be deemed to be permanently employed in such a way that the rules on termination of employment apply. For the purposes of calculating the working time after the first sentence, no deduction shall be made from the absence of the employee. § 10-5, first paragraph, last sentence is deleted. § 14-11, first paragraph, third sentence is deleted. Paragraphs 14 to 12 are replaced by the following: Paragraphs 14-12. Renting from activities for the purpose of renting Renting of workers from activities for the purpose of renting is permitted to the extent that temporary employment can be agreed under point (b) of the second paragraph of Section 14-9. [[(employee) (1)]] [[2]] In activities linked to a collective agreement with a trade union with a right of suspension, the employer and the trustees representing together a majority of the category of employees to which the lease-in relates may enter into a written agreement on fixed-term rental without prejudice to what is provided for in the first subparagraph. The establishment and the manning undertaking shall, at the request of the Labour Inspectorate, provide evidence that the activity is bound by a collective bargaining agreement with a right of suspension and that the trustees referred to in the first sentence have been agreed upon. [[3]] Only permanent employees in 100 pst. employment in a manning undertaking is entitled to the average calculation of working time on an independent basis, as set out in: Paragraphs 10 to 5. Part-time employees of temporary staff may follow the working time arrangements of the lessee enterprise Employer must at least once per year. years discuss the use of contracted labour after the first and second paragraphs, including the practice of the requirement of non-discrimination, with those elected to trust. [[4]] [[5]] In the case of rentals under this paragraph, the rules in the seventh paragraph of Sections 14 to 9 apply mutatis mutandis. [[6]] The Ministry may, by regulation, prohibit the hiring-in of certain categories of workers or in certain areas where important social considerations so warrant. [[7]] Professional association having members of an establishment which has hired workers from temporary staff undertakings may bring proceedings in its own name on the legality of such hiring. In connection with such a case, both parties will have the right to require negotiations in line with the rules of Section 17-3. the first sentence of the first paragraph of Section 18-6 is replaced by the following: The Labour Inspectorate imposes and otherwise takes the individual decisions necessary for the implementation of the provisions of Sections 1 to 7, Chapter 2 of Chapter 11 and Sections 14 to 1 (a), 14 to 5 (a), 14 to 5 (a), 14 to 9 (b), the second paragraph of the last sentence, 14 to 12 (a), 14 to 12 (a), 14 to 15 (f) and 14 to 15 (f) respectively. II The amendments shall apply from 1 July 2018.’» The Greens have already given their support to the proposal. The working party has already received subsidized support for the proposal.
